NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Fern Benally • Alberto L. Peshlakai • Jason E. Whiting • Daryl Seymore • Dawnafe Whitesinger
“We are Navajo County”
 
NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
Tuesday, August 11, 2026
NAVAJO COUNTY GOVERNMENTAL COMPLEX
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS' CHAMBERS
100 EAST CODE TALKERS DRIVE
HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
One or more supervisors may attend telephonically
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at leah.thomas@navajocountyaz.gov. 
Individuals seeking to address the Board during the Call to the Public or when an agenda item is called must fill out a
“Request to Speak” form and provide it to the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors before the meeting is called to order. Late
submissions will not be accepted. The form must indicate whether the item the person wishes to speak on is on the agenda.
 Members of the public present to speak to the Board on an agenda item must so indicate, and are asked to refrain from speaking
during the general Call to the Public.  Persons present to address agenda items will be called on during a public comment period by
the Board Chairman after the relevant agenda item is called. Individuals addressing the Board during the general Call to the Public
are limited to three minutes.  Individuals addressing the Board when an item is called are also limited to three minutes, however,
additional time may be allotted by the Chairman to address any specific questions asked by members of the Board.
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s
office at least forty-eight (48) hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.
“NOTICE TO PARENTS AND LEGAL GUARDIANS: Parents and legal guardians have the right to consent before Navajo County makes a video or voice recording
of a minor child, pursuant to A.R.S. § 1-602(A)(9). The Navajo County Board of Supervisors' regular meetings are recorded and may be viewed on Navajo County’s
website and social media pages. If you permit your child to attend/participate in a televised Navajo County Board of Supervisors meeting, a recording will be made, and
your child’s picture may be posted on Navajo County’s social media pages. You may exercise your right not to consent by not allowing your child to attend/participate
in the meeting.” 
9:00 a.m. Call To Order: Invocation and Pledge of Allegiance
  
           
1. CALL TO THE PUBLIC: Individuals may address the Board on any issue not on the agenda
and within the jurisdiction of the Board of Supervisors for up to 3 minutes.  At the close of the
call to the public, Board members may not respond to any comments but may respond to
criticism, ask staff to review a matter or ask that a matter be placed on a future agenda. 
 
2. COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: Report from County Manager, County Attorney and Board
members.

NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS’ MINUTES
Tuesday, July 14, 2026
Present: Fern Benally, Supervisor District I; Alberto Peshlakai, Supervisor
District II (appearing virtual); Jason Whiting, Supervisor District III;
Daryl Seymore, Chairman; Dawnafe Whitesinger, Vice-Chair 
Staff
Attendance: 
Bryan Layton, County Manager, Brad Carlyon, County Attorney,
Leah Thomas, Clerk of the Board 
9:02 a.m
. Call To Order:
Chairman Seymore stated that Supervisor Peshlakai was online and that Vice
Chairwoman Whitesinger and Supervisor Benally were enroute.
Invocation was shared by Chairman Seymore and the Pledge of Allegiance was led by 
Supervisor Whiting.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger arrived at 9:04 a.m. 
CALL TO THE PUBLIC:
Laura Wren expressed concern about large-scale wind and solar energy projects
proposed in the County. She stated that the Board is making decisions without public
input and that these projects are being advanced through zoning changes and
negotiations that she believes lack transparency. Laura described the potential impacts
of industrial wind turbines and extensive solar farms, including changes to the
landscape, wildlife impacts, noise, shadow flicker, and loss of open space. She stated
that these developments could lower property values and negatively affect tourism. She
emphasized that residents will bear the long-term consequences. Laura urged the Board
to place these decisions on the ballot and called on community members to attend
meetings, sign petitions, and oppose the industrialization of the county without public
consent. She concluded by encouraging public involvement and expressing her
commitment to protecting the County’s rural character.
Supervisor Benally arrived at 9:06 a.m.
Michael Pitts, speaking as a member of the White Mountain Association of Realtors,
shared concerns about the proposed Lava Run and Black Ridge energy projects. He
noted that residents and local professionals were not meaningfully included early in the
planning process. Mr. Pitts stated that the developments would permanently change the
character of the White Mountains, affecting views, recreation, tourism, and property
values in areas where people have invested heavily in homes and businesses.
He commented that the projected economic benefits do not align with what the
community expects to experience, emphasizing that most jobs would be temporary
construction work and that long-term operations are limited. He stated that landowners
with lease agreements would receive compensation while the broader community would
absorb impacts without similar benefit. Mr. Pitts also raised environmental concerns,
noting the presence of sensitive ecosystems, wildlife corridors, and water resources, and
expressing concern that once land is developed at an industrial scale, it cannot be
restored to its original condition. He said that the community has unanswered questions
regarding site selection, environmental review, mitigation plans, and long-term
accountability. He asked the Board to slow the process, ensure environmental reviews
are thorough and credible, incorporate community input, and require enforceable
1 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 8 -->
commitments related to mitigation and restoration. Mr. Pitts concluded by stating that
local realtors invest in the region and are raising concerns based on experience, while
recognizing the Board’s responsibility to balance competing interests.
Brent Meyer stated that he is a retired Chief Warrant Officer in the US Navy and resides
at 9506 Butler Trail, in unincorporated Navajo County, approximately 14 miles north and
east of Snowflake. He stated that he was speaking on behalf of more than 3,000
residents who signed a petition expressing concerns regarding Resolution 17 2025
related to sustainable energy. He asked the Board to explain the legal authority under
Arizona law that allows the planning, approval, and implementation of solar farms, wind
generators, or other renewable energy projects within unincorporated Navajo County.
He referenced several sections of Arizona Revised Statutes, the Arizona Constitution,
and Title 11 2251, noting that he does not see where these projects are authorized.
Mr. Meyer questioned whether renewable energy companies that generate and sell
electricity should be considered public utilities under Title 30 and stated that the public
deserves clear answers before additional county resources, permitting, or approvals
proceed. He also raised concerns about references to Game and Fish in the approval
process, citing Title 17 and asking for clarification on the County’s role and funding
responsibility. He stated that private landowners are required to go through planning and
zoning for development and asserted that the County has authority over land use
throughout the county. Meyer requested that the Board stop implementation of
Resolution 17 2025 and halt related activities until these legal and procedural issues are
addressed. He concluded by thanking the Board for its time and expressing appreciation
for the community and land.
Belinda Quog provided an update on two citizens’ initiative petitions. She stated that the
first petition would have required voter approval for any large-scale wind or solar farms
or battery storage units approved by the Board of Supervisors. The second petition
would have established stricter ordinances for such projects, intended to support
responsible development and protect rural heritage, scenic landscapes, dark skies,
quality of life, and property values. She said that the County Elections Director and
Deputy County Attorney imposed a signature requirement of 6,076, which she believes
conflicted with Article 4, Section 1 of the Arizona Constitution. She explained
that volunteers had 17 days to collect signatures, and that despite this, more than 3,100
residents signed the petitions. She noted that this number would have been sufficient for
a recall election and emphasized the public’s strong response and engagement.
Ms. Quog stated that the signatures represented broad public disappointment regarding
transparency, communication, and the Board’s handling of large-scale industrial
projects. She expressed appreciation for the volunteers and residents who participated.
Chairman Seymore asked the gallery to maintain decorum.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger asked County Attorney Carlyon if the Board can
respond to criticism during the Call to the Public.
Louise Liggett expressed concerns regarding wind turbine development in the county.
She stated her belief that wind turbines are ineffective, costly, and harmful to the
landscape, wildlife, and air quality. Ms. Liggett questioned the reasoning behind
supporting these projects and expressed frustration about their potential impact on the
community. She referred specifically to the Native American members of the Board and
asked how they could support projects that she believes conflict with protecting local
land, hunting, fishing, and natural resources. Ms. Liggett stated that she felt the Board
                                                                    2 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 9 -->
had not been transparent and expressed concern about effects on water resources and
the involvement of outside investors. She urged the Board to reconsider its position,
stating that the projects do not reflect responsible stewardship of the land.
Robert Black stated he was speaking as a member of the Holbrook City Council and
raised concerns regarding the recently approved Flock camera system. He said
residents had asked him about the approval of the cameras, and he learned about them
only after investigating the matter himself. Black noted that nearly a half million dollars
was allocated for 47-49 cameras and questioned the placement of at least one camera
on McClaws Rd., a rural road, as well as the overall distribution of the cameras. He
stated that when he contacted both the Holbrook Chief of Police and the Sheriff’s Office,
neither agency was aware of the cameras or how to access the data collected by them.
Black questioned who currently has access to the information gathered and expressed
concern that individuals outside of law enforcement may be able to access the data. He
said he has seen other communities cancel their contracts for similar systems and urged
the Board to reconsider and discontinue its contract with Flock, stating that the cameras
are unnecessary for the community.
Donna Wastchak, a 31 year resident of Pinetop Lakeside, requested that the Board add
a future agenda item to reopen discussion on Resolution 17 2025. She stated her belief
that placing the item on a public agenda would allow residents to speak openly, improve
transparency, and help the public understand why the resolution was adopted, including
decisions related to setbacks for large-scale renewable energy projects. Ms. Wastchak
expressed concern that the resolution created conditions favorable for large-scale wind
projects while limiting public input from county residents. She stated that the county’s
scenic landscapes and rural character are important to constituents and referenced the
visibility of existing turbines. She shared concerns about the height of turbines, water
use during construction, and impacts to the regional aquifer, noting that some wells
in Pinetop-Lakeside have already begun to run dry. She also referenced recent
water-related concerns within local reservations and questioned how additional industrial
use of water might affect the shared resources. Ms. Wastchak stated that by approving
the resolution, the Board opened opportunities for outside developers while making
residents feel excluded. She emphasized the Board’s responsibility to its approximately
107,000 constituents and noted the county’s long-standing local heritage. She
concluded by expressing disappointment in the Board’s actions and reiterated her
request to place Resolution 17 2025 on a future agenda for open public discussion.
Liz Olmstead expressed concerns about large-scale renewable energy projects
proposed in Navajo County and the transparency of related processes. She stated that
recent setback changes were made voluntarily by a developer rather than through
Board action. She noted community frustration over delayed public records requests and
cited several Arizona Revised Statutes she believes have been violated. Ms. Olmstead
requested timely completion of outstanding records requests, public posting of
calendars, and disclosure of related financial activity. She urged the Board to pause
approval of industrial projects and consider a moratorium.
Vice Chair Whitesinger asked for clarification on the amount of signatures required for
the recently circulated citizens initiatives.
County Attorney Brad Carlyon responded that the Arizona Statute sets the percentage of
signatures based upon the last gubernatorial election and the timeframe for the deadline
                                                                    3 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 10 -->
on when a petition can be filed. He further clarified that the Elections Director and
Deputy County Attorney did not set the date that the petitions needed to be filed, it was
set by statute and constitution. 
Vice Chair Whitesinger thanked Mr. Carlyon and stated that the amount of signature
required was not dictated by Navajo County but dictated by the state and that Navajo
County was following process.  She further explained the amount of days to gather the
signatures was also following the letter of the law.  She asked Mr. Carlyon what the
percentage required was. 
Mr. Carlyon replied that for county initiatives the signatures needed is 15% of the total
votes cast at the last gubernatorial election.
Vice Chair Whitesinger thanked Mr. Carlyon and acknowledged that the citizens do
their research and that she wants to provide the most accurate information. 
Supervisor Whiting clarified that he did not make a statement about Cholla becoming
nuclear. However, he did attend the corporation commission and testified about the
possibilities of nuclear.  He further clarified that APS made the announcement about
Cholla being converted and that Navajo County shared the press releases. He then
stated that the owners of the land are able to make decisions about the use of their land.
Brad Carlyon advised the Board that during the Call to the Public they should only reply
to criticisms. 
Supervisor Whiting stated that he thinks it is important for the public to be informed
and referred to the new community information page on the Navajo County website. He
then clarified that the Lava Run and Black Ridge projects referenced in the Call to the
Public are in Apache County.
Chairman Seymore invited the public to look at the Navajo County Information Center
page on the website stating that it has a lot of information on the steps that the Board
has taken and answers many questions. He then clarified that installing a turbine does
not require 60,000 gallons of water, but instead approximately 400 yards of concrete,
not 1,875 yards. He noted that renewable energy is no longer among the most
expensive energy sources; while costs may have been higher five years ago,
renewables are now among the least expensive options. He thanked participants for
their input and requested that staff review any legal questions raised during the meeting
and provide clarification through the Information Center. He also explained that a Flock
camera is a license plate reader.
 
COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: 
Supervisor Whiting reported that two weeks ago he sent a letter to members of
Congress asking them to review federal tax credits and subsidies on renewable
projects.  He reported that Staff had found subsidies that still exist. He explained that
utility companies have all resource RFPs out and that a power purchase agreement with
a utility company must be in place prior to coming into the state of Arizona. He
encouraged the public to investigate the subsidies that still exist and to write to
Congress asking for them to be removed. He then stated that he sent a letter to state
legislators asking them to consider removing the 80% tax subsidy on property taxes. He
then shared his excitement with the announcement that Cholla is converting to natural
                                                                    4 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 11 -->
gas, sharing that it will create jobs in the county and there are other future opportunities
that could come.  He then reported that he and Chairman Seymore toured the Novo
Bio Power facility, and he appreciated getting to share with the Chairman some
knowledge about the facility and some of the jobs that it provides and how it helps with
forest restoration and providing some power to the region. He thanked the Board for
their support with the Cholla announcement, noting that it was a 10-year process that
involved many people throughout the communities of Navajo County.
Chairman Seymore reported on the recent NACOG meeting, which included discussion
on regional economic development. He noted several initiatives underway, including the
City of Show Low’s incubator project involving renovation of a 20,000 square foot former
grade school. The city received a $275,000 grant to support this effort. Workforce
housing remains a significant challenge and is largely dependent on private sector
solutions. He shared that Flagstaff currently faces a housing shortage estimated at
between 52,000 and 57,000 units and that housing costs in the area are approximately
50% above the national average. The NACOG meeting also provided updates regarding
tribal partnerships, broadband investments, improvements to health-related data
systems, and activities with both state and federal transportation boards. Chairman
Seymore shared federal legislative updates related to the Build America Act. He
announced that the Transportation Policy Summit will take place October 14-16 in
Flagstaff, where regional concerns related to roads and transportation projects will be
presented. He expressed hope that everyone enjoyed the Fourth of July, noting his
participation in local celebrations in Heber-Overgaard and Pinedale/Clay Springs.
Chairman Seymore reflected briefly on the nation’s history, mentioning Thomas
Paine’s pamphlet “Common Sense” and its influence on public sentiment leading up to
the Declaration of Independence. He discussed the desire for religious freedom among
early settlers and the lasting impact of values in American culture. He also shared a
historical note on Paine’s later years and how his beliefs led to controversy and social
isolation. Chairman Seymore concluded by emphasizing community unity, the
importance of working together, and respecting lawful property rights. He thanked
attendees for their participation.

ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES: 
a. Presentation to Darcey McKee on her Retirement from Navajo County after 36
Years of Service.
Eric Scott recognized Ms. McKee for her 36 years of service stating that she is
loyal to Navajo Conty and serves the residents of Winslow with a smile.
Amy Stradling recognized Ms. McKee for her 36 years of dedicated service to
Navajo County. She shared that throughout her career, she demonstrated
professionalism, compassion, and a strong commitment to public health, with a
particular passion for the Tobacco Program. Her efforts have significantly
improved community health and will positively impact the county for years to
come. Ms. Stradling shard that Ms. McKee is known for her willingness to step
in whenever needed, consistently supporting teammates, mentoring others,
and assisting her hometown of Winslow. She is regarded as dependable,
compassionate, and a true team player.
Darcey McKee stated that in her 36 years with Navajo County, she only
worked in the Public Health department and that she has enjoyed her job.  She
shared that she is proud to stand behind Navajo County and that in retirement
she looks forward to future volunteer work within Navajo County.
 
b. Update on the APS announcement for the Cholla Power Plant Conversion.
A presentation was shown.
Rochelle Lacapa provided an update on the announcement from APS that it
plans to convert two units at Cholla from coal to Natural Gas with operations
expected to begin in 2029.  She stated that the conversion process will
preserve Cholla as a critical part of Arizona’s energy grid.  She then explained
that it’s significance to Navajo County will be providing jobs, funding for schools
and public services and reinforces years of collaboration between the County
and APS. She explained that the two converted units will add roughly 300
megawatts of power serving approximately 60,000 homes statewide, supporting
around the clock reliability and quick response to peak demand.  Ms. Lacapa
                                                                    7 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 14 -->
stated that it is estimated that the project will create hundreds of construction
jobs and dozens of permanent positions once the conversion is operational,
adding that the continued operations will create local revenue for Navajo
County. She reported that APS plans to use existing transmission
infrastructure, only adding a small lateral pipeline. She reported that
construction is expected to begin in 2028 pending regulatory approvals and that
open houses, outreach and public engagement will happen throughout 20206
to gather community input. Ms. Lacapa highlighted the consistent partnership
between APS and Navajo County throughout the process. 
Supervisor Whiting thanked the Board, staff, and community members for
their ongoing discussions and engagement on the issue. He noted that APS
has listened to community voices over the years and expressed optimism about
opportunities related to existing infrastructure, especially for the Joseph
City community and its school, which rely heavily on it. He highlighted that as
APS works to double its power production over the next decade, new options
may emerge for future development within the region. He emphasized that this
area has long played a significant role in Arizona’s power generation and that
APS recognizes the contributions of local communities. Supervisor Whiting
expressed appreciation to APS for keeping the County involved in
conversations when appropriate and acknowledged the importance of
conveying how vital the issue is for the region moving forward. He concluded
by expressing gratitude to the communities for their continued dialogue and to
APS for the jobs it provides locally, and he thanked everyone for their
leadership on the matter.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger congratulated APS on its efforts to provide an
additional energy resource for Arizona. She noted the significance of powering
approximately 60,000 homes and emphasized the growing energy demands
driven by an increasing population and reliance on electricity. She highlighted
that consumers depend on energy for nearly every aspect of daily life, including
public safety, schools, healthcare facilities, and other essential services,
especially in a desert environment where responsible resource management is
critical. She shared a personal reflection about her father, who lived for many
years without electricity and only recently gained access. She expressed
gratitude to organizations like APS and for all partners working to develop
energy solutions that meet the needs of Arizona’s growing population.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger concluded by thanking APS and those
involved for their efforts and congratulating them on this achievement.
Chairman Seymore reflected on the effort involved in building and maintaining
the facility, noting the significant manpower, planning, design, and time
invested over many decades. He shared that Unit 1, built in 1962, is still
operating at 99% efficiency. He explained that Units 1 and 3 will continue
operating, while Unit 2 will not and Unit 4 is not owned by APS. He thanked
staff for their work and acknowledged Supervisor Whiting for his efforts within
the district. Chairman Seymore emphasized the positive impact this project will
have on revitalizing Joseph City. He also noted the importance of balancing
traditional energy generation with renewable sources-recognizing that while the
facility will continue to use water, renewable energy sources do not consume
water once built, making the combined approach valuable. He shared
                                                                    8 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 15 -->
comments from APS leadership, noting that the company, in its 140-year
history, took 130 years to reach a certain level of production, then had to double
that output within a decade, and expects to double it again within the next ten
years due to rapidly increasing statewide energy demand. Chairman
Seymore expressed pride in APS, SRP, and Novo Power, stating that these
developments represent significant wins for Navajo County.
Supervisor Benally recalled that discussions about the facility have been
ongoing for several years, including conversations held after the Cholla plant
was slated for closure. She noted meeting with Senator Gallego to
discuss potential changes and said the outcome now taking shape aligns with
what was anticipated. She expressed appreciation for APS, noting that many
families on the reservation rely solely on solar power, which can be
unreliable-especially at night and during winter months. Without access to wind
power and with limited solar capacity, households often face overnight outages
and must prepare alternative heating sources for their homes. She emphasized
that many residents prefer full electric service over solar because interruptions
significantly affect daily activities. Supervisor Benally thanked APS for its efforts
and also recognized County staff, including the County Manager and Rochelle,
for their work and involvement during key discussions. She then asked whether
the new project would create as many permanent jobs as the former coal fired
plant?
Ms. Lacapa answered that there would not be as many jobs provided as there
were before decommissioning of the coal plant.
Chairman Seymore invited Robert Black to speak on the agenda item. 
Robert Black expressed appreciation to Supervisor Whiting for explaining that
it was a community effort to work with APS on the announcement.  He
suggested that APS look into obtaining the boiler from the old papermill to use
in the conversion.
 
PUBLIC FIDUCIARY:
a. Annual Report to the Board on department activities.
A presentation was shown
Eileen Rogers provided an update on the Public Fiduciary Office, noting she
has served as director for one year.  She explained that Fiduciaries are
court-appointed by the Superior Court to protect and support the county’s most
vulnerable residents, including elderly individuals, persons with developmental
disabilities, and those with severe mental illness. Responsibilities include
safeguarding client assets, coordinating assisted-living placements, ensuring
medication and care needs are met, and maintaining regular in-person visits.
Ms. Rogers explained that the office has had significant turnover, leaving a
largely new staff that require extensive training. She has organized in-house
training, invited partner agencies to provide instruction, and coordinated virtual
training with experts from other states. She shared that Fiduciaries must have
three years of experience and pass a state licensing exam, which no longer
includes formal state training. She emphasized the importance of consistent
record-keeping through the EMS system and described the complexity of
decision-making involved in client care where there are often multiple providers
                                                                    9 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 16 -->
working together to help make decisions. Ms. Rogers shared several examples
illustrating the challenges and rewards of fiduciary work, including a client
whose driving privileges were removed for safety reasons, a young client who
regained limited mobility through coordinated agency support, and efforts to
reunite clients with family members using technology and DNA tools. She noted
that Eileen Rogers strengthening partnerships between agencies is essential to
meeting clients’ needs. The office participates in annual state and national
fiduciary conferences to continue education and improve services. Ms. Rogers
concluded by reaffirming the office’s focus on ensuring clients receive the
highest possible quality of life and invited questions from the Board.
Chairman Seymore expressed appreciation to Ms. Rogers for the work and
service she and her team provide, noting that it can be difficult work at times.
He asked Ms. Rogers to let her staff know that they are appreciated.   
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger thanked Ms. Rogers.
 
RECORDER:
a. Update on upcoming Elections
A presentation was shown 
Recorder David Marshall provided an overview of the responsibilities of the
Recorder’s Office. He shared that duties include recording and preserving
official public documents, maintaining accurate voter registration records,
designating and removing early voting locations, and administering all early
voting operations. He noted that the office is frequently mistaken for the
Elections Department, though the two are separate. Mr. Marshall reported that
two ballot drop box locations were removed this year due to tampering and
extremely low usage. The office is identifying new, more secure and more
frequented locations.
He shared current voter registration statistics: Navajo County has 78,207
registered voters, including 5,275 classified as inactive due to undeliverable
election mail and lack of response to follow up notices. He reported that there
is a current issue with early ballots being returned undeliverable; more than
900 ballots have come back, prompting widespread voter frustration. He has
contacted multiple post offices and the Phoenix Postmaster to determine why
ballots are not reaching local post offices for delivery. He clarified that “postage
due” labels reflect a second postal charge for returned mail, not unpaid bills.
The office mailed 800 letters to affected voters explaining the issue and
informing them of options to still vote, including early voting at the Recorder’s
Office through July 17 or voting at their polling place on Election Day, July 21.
He also shared that inactive voters may still vote if they present proper
identification.
Mr. Marshall reviewed cancellation statistics: 658 registrations have been
canceled this year for reasons such as death, felony convictions, moving out of
the county, or voter request. He reported that staff continue working to maintain
accurate voter rolls. Next, he shared that the Recorder’s Office will soon launch
a “Recorder’s Corner” webpage on the County website which will be updated
regularly to improve transparency and provide clear explanations of office
procedures. He reported that early voting participation is low: 121 in person
early voters and 4,604 early ballots returned by mail, along with 1,463 ballots
returned through ballot boxes. The large number of undeliverable ballots
                                                                    10 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 17 -->
remains a serious concern. Looking ahead, the office is coordinating with other
states to identify duplicate registrations and ensure accuracy. He concluded by
stating that early in-person voting ends July 17, and early ballots must be
delivered by 7:00 p.m. on Election Day, July 21. He shared that staff will work
extended hours and overtime as needed to keep up with increasing workload.
Recorder Marshall concluded his report and stated that he and Deputy
Recorder Jose Lerma are available for questions. 
Supervisor Whiting thanked Mr. Marshall and the team at the Recorder’s
Office for the report and for their diligence.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger thanked Mr. Marshall and Mr. Lerma for the
presentation and information provided. 
Supervisor Benally expressed appreciation to the Recorder’s Office and
Election department and said that she is looking forward to the Recorder’s
Corner. 
Recorder Marshall shared that his office has an open door policy and that he is
always available. 
Chairman Seymore expressed his appreciation for the presentation and
transparency presented, stating that he will be curious to see how many
personal letters get returned.
 
PUBLIC WORKS:
a. Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 19-2026 granting
a right-of-way exchange of a portion of "South Meadow Road" in Heber.
Will Flake presented a request for the Board’s consideration regarding the
possible adoption of Resolution 19 2026, which would approve a right-of-way
exchange for a portion of South Meadow Road in Heber, within the
Buckskin/Artist Draw community development area. He explained that a portion
of the road was not originally constructed within the designated right-of-way,
resulting in the existing alignment crossing through portions of three separate
parcels. He stated that previous applications to correct this issue were
submitted, but the necessary survey work was never completed. A property
owner has now completed the required survey, allowing the county to proceed.
He explained that the proposal would abandon the current misaligned
right-of-way located on private properties and replace it with a new right-of-way
along the west side, where the road is constructed and tied into church property
to the south.
Mr. Flake recommended approval of the right-of-way exchange as outlined in
the updated application.
Supervisor Whiting made a motion to approve Resolution Number 19-2026
granting a right-of-way exchange of a portion of "South Meadow Road" in
Heber, motion seconded by Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger. Vote 4-0
approving the motion with Supervisor Peshlakai not voting.

Will Flake presented a request for the Board’s consideration regarding the
proposed abandonment of a portion of Goodwill Lane, located north and west of
the Larson and Hilltop intersection in Lakeside. The request would abandon
approximately 185 feet at the west end of the road. He noted that the area is
fully developed and that all properties will retain their existing access; the
abandonment will not impact any property owner’s ability to reach their home or
parcel. He explained that adjacent property owners have submitted letters
supporting the proposal. Mr. Flake emphasized that the action pertains only to
abandoning the right-of-way itself and that any utilities located within the area
are separate matters to be addressed by the property owner in coordination
with the utility companies.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger made a motion to approve Resolution
Number 20-2026 granting a right-of-way abandonment and sale of a portion of
"Goodwill Lane" in Lakeside, motion seconded by Supervisor Whiting. Vote
4-0 approving the motion with Supervisor Peshlakai not voting.

REGULAR AGENDA: 
a. Consideration of a Land Purchase Agreement between Ronda C. Folland and
Christina E. Flemming (Sellers) and Navajo County, Arizona (Buyer) for Flood
Control in the Linden Area, Navajo County, Arizona.
Donald Perkins reported a drainage issue affecting Parcel 409 73 034, located
in Linden Trails, and owned by Rhonda Foland and Christina Fleming. He
explained that as the lowest lot on the north side of the street, the parcel
receives significant flood runoff through its center and at its southwest corner,
functioning almost naturally as a catch basin. Linden Trails experiences heavy
runoff through county right-of-way and neighboring properties. Mr. Perkins
explained that J2 Engineering, the firm that designed the Linden Trails drainage
project, conducted an analysis and recommended that the County purchase
the 0.58 acre parcel, stating that it is an ideal location for a detention facility,
                                                                    12 of 14 
Draft

<!-- page 19 -->
which would help detain and slowly release stormwater, reduce flood risk for
surrounding residents, and improve overall community flood safety.
Chairman Seymore asked if a plat amendment was required and if the
homeowner’s association had given approval.
Mr. Perkins stated that no plat amendment was needed and that the
homeowner’s association had given approval. 
Will Flake confirmed that no plat amendment was needed. 
Chairman Seymore noted that work is ongoing to address drainage concerns
in the Linden Trails area. He emphasized that the proposed parcel acquisition
and planned detention basin represent only part of the overall solution but
would improve the ability to redirect and manage runoff north of the
neighborhood. He expressed appreciation to staff for their continued efforts and
for the numerous meetings held with the homeowner’s association to address
community concerns.
Chairman Seymore made a motion to approve a Land Purchase Agreement
between Ronda C. Folland and Christina E. Flemming (Sellers) and Navajo
County, Arizona (Buyer) for Flood Control in the Linden Area, Navajo County,
Arizona, motion seconded by Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion.

NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS’ MINUTES
Tuesday, July 30, 2026
Present: Fern Benally, Supervisor District I (appearing virtual); Jason Whiting,
Supervisor District III (appearing virtual); Daryl Seymore, Chairman
(appearing virtual); Dawnafe Whitesinger, Vice-Chair (appearing
virtual) 
Absent: Alberto Peshlakai, Supervisor District II 
Staff
Attendance: 
Bryan Layton, County Manager, Jason Moore, Deputy County
Attorney (appearing vitual), Rayleen Richards, Elections Director
(appearing virtual), Leah Thomas, Clerk of the Board (appearing
virtual) 
9:02 a.m
. Call To Order: Chairman Seymore stated that Vice Chairwoman
Whitesinger, Supervisor Benally, and Supervisor Whiting were present online. 
ELECTIONS:
a. Consideration and approval of the Official Canvass of the results for the July 21,
2026, Primary Election as presented.
Rayleen Richards provided an update on recent election activities conducted by
the Elections Office. She reported that on Saturday, the Elections Office
conducted the required hand count audit of the primary election. The hand count
was performed by a bipartisan team consisting of six Democrats and six
Republicans. Representatives from both parties met on July 15th for the initial
drawing to determine which races and early ballot batches would be included in
the audit. Because the number of early ballots was not yet known at that time and
state law requires auditing either one percent of early ballots or two batches of
100; whichever is greater; more batches were drawn than were ultimately
needed. Parties also drew two Democratic races and three Republican races.
Following the close of the election on July 22nd, a second drawing was held to
narrow down the early ballot batches. This resulted in selecting two batches of
100 early ballots, along with determining which two vote centers would be
included in the hand count. Ms. Richards noted that the audit team did
outstanding work and reported that the hand-count results were 100 percent
accurate. These results are publicly available on the Arizona Secretary of State’s
website. Ms. Richards also stated that on Monday, the Elections Office
conducted the post Logic and Accuracy (L&A) test with Lisa Green present on
behalf of the Republican Party. The Logic and Accuracy test results were 100
percent accurate. She informed the Board that the Elections Office is currently
testing a new election night reporting site, and a link to the updated reporting
system is available on the Elections page on the county website. Ms. Richards
requested that the Board approve the results of the July 21, 2026, Primary
Election as presented.
Chairman Seymore expressed appreciation to the Elections staff for their
diligent work throughout the Primary Election period. He noted that during early
voting, Navajo County experienced flooding that resulted in the closure of the
1 of 3 
Draft

<!-- page 22 -->
County Complex. He commended their efforts during early voting. He also
referenced an incident involving a lock box where a key had been inadvertently
left. He stated that the matter had been investigated to confirm that no additional
ballots were cast, and that ballot integrity was maintained. He thanked staff for
addressing the issue thoroughly and responsibly. He observed that voter turnout
totaled just under 21,000 ballots cast out of approximately 72,000 registered
voters. While turnout was not high, he expressed gratitude for the election
process and those who participate in it and serve the community.
Supervisor Benally made a motion to approve the Official Canvass of the
results for the July 21, 2026, Primary Election as presented, motion seconded by
Supervisor Whiting.  Vote 4-0 approving the motion with Supervisor Peshlakai 
absent. 
At 9:10 a.m. the meeting was adjourned
At 9:15 a.m. Chairman Seymore reconvened the meeting explaining that a
quorum had been met with Supervisor Peshlakai, Supervisor Whiting, and
Vice Chair Whitesinger online. 
Chairman Seymore stated that there were citizens present who would like to
make Public Comment.
Jerry Hubbard from Overgaard addressed the Board stating that he has been a
poll worker since the Primary Election of 2022, serving as a Marshal at the
Heber-Overgaard location.  He shared that at the end of the day on the July 21,
2026, Primary Election, Inspector Ralph Roberts asked him to help carry out 3
voting booths and a plastic bin which contained approximately 3000 ballots.
Chairman Seymore asked Mr. Hubbard if his comment had anything to do with
the 2026 Primary Election.
Mr. Hubbard said that the incident happened during this election. 
Chairman Seymore asked if legal counsel was present.  The Clerk contacted
legal counsel to attend the meeting. 
At 9:19 a.m. Jason Moore joined the meeting. 
At 9:19 a.m. Supervisor Benally joined the meeting. 
Chairman Seymore explained that the meeting was reconvened because there
were citizens present who had requested to speak. He explained that they were
asking about the unvoted ballots that were discovered from 2024.  He said that
the matter was not an agenda item and asked Mr. Moore if the speaker should
continue. 
Jason Moore stated that if speakers wanted to discuss 2024 ballots discovered
during the 2026 Primary, he suggested that they make a public comment to the
Board at the next meeting, explaining that the current meeting is for canvassing
the 2026 Primary Election. He further explained that the comments are beyond
the scope of the current meeting and respectfully asked that the public
                                                                    2 of 3

commentors come to the next meeting.

Donna Wastchak addressed the Board, stating she worked at the Whiteriver
polling location and brought five friends who had registered to work elections but
had not been called. She further explained that the party had called the Elections
Office on the Friday before election day and been granted the opportunity to
work at the Whiteriver location.  She stated that illegal activity at the center was
observed, including: ID Judges letting people receive ballot tickets by vouching
for them and the Inspector taking early ballots without checking ID.  She reported
that a couple brought in their mail-in ballots not voted and asked for new ballots,
stating that she took the mail-in ballots to destroy, however the other ballot judge
insisted that the couple vote using their mail-in ballots.

and that she is handicapped and not able to easily travel to a vote center.

Re: Comments on Proposed Rule – Regulation for Federal Financial Assistance; 2 CFR Part 200 (Docket 
OMB-2026-0034) 
Dear Director Vought: 
On behalf of the Navajo County Board of Supervisors, I respectfully submit these comments regarding the 
Oﬃce of Management and Budget's proposed revisions to 2 CFR Part 200, commonly referred to as the 
Uniform Guidance. 
Navajo County appreciates and supports the goals of transparency, accountability, stewardship of taxpayer 
resources, and prevention of waste, fraud, and abuse in federal ﬁnancial assistance programs. We recognize 
the importance of ensuring that federal fu nds are expended in accordance with federal law and program 
objectives. At the same time, we urge OMB to carefully consider the practical impacts that several 
provisions of the proposed rule will have on rural county governments and the communities they serve. 
Navajo County administers and participates in numerous federally funded programs supporting public 
safety, public health, emergency management, transportation infrastructure, behavioral health services, 
victim services, workforce development, and other ess ential governmental functions. Federal funding 
supports services that are critical to residents throughout our county, including many residents living in 
remote and geographically isolated communities. 
Navajo County covers nearly 10,000 square miles, making it one of the largest counties in Arizona by land 
area. Providing services across such a large geographic region presents unique operational challenges. The 
County serves numerous unincorporated commu nities, remote rural populations, and works closely with 
tribal governments and tribal communities throughout northeastern Arizona. The cost of delivering 
services across this vast geographic area is substantial, and federal funding frequently provides the  
resources necessary to ensure residents receive basic governmental services regardless of where they live. 
Unlike larger metropolitan jurisdictions, Navajo County has a limited local tax base available to absorb 
unexpected funding disruptions or to replace lost federal funds. Consequently, federal grant programs often 
provide resources that cannot realistically  be replicated through local revenue sources. For that reason, 
changes that increase uncertainty regarding the availability or continuity of federal funding have a 
disproportionately signiﬁcant impact on rural counties such as Navajo County. 
Concerns Regarding Expanded Discretionary Termination Authority 
Navajo County is particularly concerned by the proposed expansion and clariﬁcation of discretionary grant 
termination authority contained in Sections 200.340 and 200.341. 
The proposed rule would authorize federal agencies to terminate discretionary awards when an agency 
determines that an award no longer advances agency priorities, program goals, or the national interest. 
While we recognize the federal government's legitimate interest in ensuring federal awards remain aligned 
with statutory purposes and executive branch priorities, the proposed standard creates substantial 
uncertainty for local governments responsible for implementing long-term programs. 
County governments routinely make multi-year commitments based upon approved federal awards. Such 
commitments frequently include hiring employees, entering into contracts, procuring equipment,

<!-- page 26 -->
implementing infrastructure projects, and establishing service delivery systems intended to operate 
throughout the duration of an award period. 
During Fiscal Year 2025, Navajo County received approximately $11.7 million in federal grant funding. 
Those funds support approximately 1 8 percent of the County's workforce (1 24.62 of 686 .82 full-time 
equivalent positions) and help provide essential governmental services throughout one of Arizona's largest 
and most rural counties. These federally funded positions include professionals working in public safety, 
public health, emergency managem ent, transportation, behavioral health, victim services, infrastructure 
development, and other essential governmental functions. Because many of these positions and programs 
cannot be supported through local revenues alone, interruptions in federal funding would result in staﬃng 
reductions, delayed project completion, diminished public safety and public health capabilities, and 
reduced services to County residents.  
Unlike larger metropolitan jurisdictions, Navajo County does not have the tax base necessary to absorb the 
sudden loss of multimillion-dollar federal awards or replace federally funded personnel and infrastructure 
projects with local revenues. Rural counti es necessarily make long -term staﬃng, contracting, and capital 
investment decisions in reliance upon approved federal grant awards. The proposed expansion of 
discretionary termination authority fundamentally alters that reliance by introducing uncertaint y into 
programs and projects that often require years of planning and implementation. 
Federal funding is particularly critical for large -scale infrastructure projects that far exceed the ﬁnancial 
capacity of rural counties. For example, the Winslow Levee Project, funded through the U.S. Army Corps 
of Engineers, is a ﬂood -risk reduction pr oject exceeding $100 million. The project encompasses 
approximately 4.3 miles along the Little Colorado River and is expected to protect approximately 1,600 
structures while removing nearly 2,700 parcels from the high-risk ﬂoodplain. The County has undertaken 
this project in reliance upon an approved federal funding commitment. If funding were interrupted after 
engineering, environmental review, contracting, and construction activities had begun, the consequences 
would extend well beyond a delayed completion date. The County could face increased construction costs, 
contractual liabilities, project ineﬃciencies, and continued ﬂood risk for residents and businesses awaiting 
completion of this critical public safety infrastructure. 
Federal funding is also enabling Navajo County to construct a 106 -mile open -access middle -mile 
broadband ﬁber network connecting Joseph City, Holbrook, Snowﬂake, Taylor, Show Low, Pinetop -
Lakeside, Heber-Overgaard, and the White Mountain Apache Tribe. The project will signiﬁcantly improve 
broadband availability, telehealth, educational opportunities, public safety communications, emergency 
response capabilities, and economic development throughout northeastern Arizona. The open- access 
backbone will enable last-mile providers, municipalities, and telecommunications carriers to connect more 
than 1,300 businesses and surrounding communities to major regional internet hubs. Like other major 
infrastructure projects, broadband deployment requires years of planning, engineering, permitting, 
procurement, and construction. Local governments cannot responsibly undertake projects of this 
m a g n i t u d e  i f  a p p r o v e d  f e d e r a l  f u n d i n g  m a y  l a t e r  b e  w i t h d r a w n  f o r  r e a s o n s  u n r e l a t e d  t o  p r o j e c t  
performance or grant compliance. 
Federal grant funding also supports numerous public safety, public health, and community resiliency 
initiatives throughout Navajo County, including public safety communications systems, overdose 
prevention and engagement programs, behavioral health partner ships, emergency management 
performance, transportation improvements, road and sidewalk construction, and other essential 
governmental services. These programs represent collaborative partnerships among local, state, federal, 
tribal, nonproﬁt, and private-sector organizations. They advance not only local priorities but also important 
federal objectives related to public safety, disaster preparedness, public health, infrastructure resilience, 
and economic opportunity. Increased uncertainty surrounding federal grant funding may discourage rural 
counties from pursuing innovative programs or making the long -term investments necessary to achieve 
those shared objectives.

<!-- page 27 -->
The prospect of mid-award termination based upon changing policy priorities creates signiﬁcant ﬁscal and 
operational uncertainty. Counties must develop annual budgets, workforce plans, and service commitments 
months in advance. Local governments cannot r esponsibly recruit employees, enter into contracts, or 
undertake long -term projects if funding remains subject to termination for reasons unrelated to 
performance, compliance, or misuse of funds. 
The workforce implications are particularly concerning. Rural counties already face signiﬁcant challenges 
recruiting and retaining qualiﬁed personnel, including attorneys, engineers, nurses, behavioral health 
professionals, emergency managers, grant admi nistrators, and law enforcement personnel. Potential 
employees are understandably reluctant to accept positions if funding sources may be terminated before 
the completion of the grant period. The proposed rule may therefore impair the ability of rural counties to 
recruit and retain qualiﬁed personnel necessary to implement federally funded programs. 
Accordingly, Navajo County urges OMB to: 
• Limit discretionary termination authority to clearly deﬁned circumstances; 
• Establish objective criteria governing termination decisions; 
• Require meaningful notice and an opportunity to cure where appropriate; 
• Provide adequate wind-down periods; 
• Ensure reimbursement for unavoidable termination costs incurred in good-faith reliance upon approved 
federal awards. 
Concerns Regarding Expanded Pass-Through Entity Responsibilities 
Navajo County is also concerned regarding the proposed expansion of pass-through entity responsibilities 
and oversight obligations. 
Many federally funded programs rely upon partnerships among counties, nonproﬁt organizations, 
healthcare providers, educational institutions, tribal entities, and community -based organizations. The 
proposed rule would substantially increase monitoring, re porting, documentation, and compliance 
obligations imposed upon pass-through entities. 
While accountability is important, these expanded requirements create additional administrative burdens 
that will require counties to devote scarce personnel and ﬁnancial resources toward compliance activities 
rather than direct service delivery. 
For rural counties, these requirements function as an unfunded mandate. Compliance responsibilities may 
require: 
• Additional grant management personnel; 
• Additional legal review; 
• Additional auditing and monitoring activities; 
• New reporting systems; 
• Additional contract administration. 
Every dollar spent on compliance administration is a dollar unavailable for direct services to residents. 
Navajo County is particularly concerned that the increased compliance burden may discourage 
participation by smaller nonproﬁt organizations and community -based providers that lack the 
administrative capacity necessary to satisfy expanded federal requireme nts. This concern is especially 
signiﬁcant in rural communities where service providers are already limited. 
Concerns Regarding Event Services Requirements 
The proposed event -services and viewpoint -neutrality provisions raise signiﬁcant legal and practical 
concerns for local governments.

<!-- page 28 -->
Navajo County owns and operates various public facilities that are routinely used for meetings, public 
gatherings, community events, educational activities, governmental functions, and other public purposes. 
Applying federal grant conditions to activities occurring on county-owned property regardless of whether 
federal funds support the event itself may expose counties to increased legal risk while creating substantial 
uncertainty regarding implementation and enforcement. 
The County urges OMB to limit these requirements to activities directly supported by federal funding and 
to provide clear guidance regarding implementation responsibilities for local governments. 
Concerns Regarding Reclassiﬁcation of Subtitle A as Binding Federal Regulation 
Navajo County is also concerned regarding the proposal to transform 2 CFR Subtitle A from guidance into 
a directly binding federal regulation applicable government-wide without separate agency implementation. 
The proposed Uniform Grants Regulation would signiﬁcantly centralize federal grant administration 
authority within OMB and permit future government- wide changes to become eﬀective immediately 
following OMB rulemaking. 
Although the County recognizes OMB's desire for consistency and uniformity, the proposal raises concerns 
regarding federal preemption of local grant administration and the ability of counties to eﬀectively plan for 
future regulatory changes. 
Counties administer thousands of pages of federal grant requirements across multiple federal agencies. The 
proposed structure may result in future grant administration requirements changing simultaneously across 
all programs with limited time for local governments to evaluate impacts, revise policies, update contracts, 
train personnel, and implement compliance measures. 
T h e  C o u n t y  r e s p e c t f u l l y  r e q u e s t s  t h a t  O M B  p r e s e r v e  m e a n i n g f u l  o p p o r t u n i t i e s  f o r  s t a t e  a n d  l o c a l  
governments to participate in future rulemaking processes aﬀecting federal ﬁnancial assistance programs 
and provide suﬃcient implementation periods before major regulatory changes become eﬀective. 
Impact on Rural Counties 
More broadly, Navajo County urges OMB to carefully evaluate the cumulative impact of the proposed rule 
on rural jurisdictions. 
Individually, many of the proposed provisions may appear manageable. Collectively, however, they 
signiﬁcantly increase administrative complexity, compliance obligations, reporting requirements, oversight 
responsibilities, and funding uncertainty. 
Large metropolitan governments may possess the personnel, legal departments, grant oﬃces, and 
administrative infrastructure necessary to absorb these additional requirements. Rural counties often do 
not. 
As a result, the proposed rule risks creating a grant administration environment in which rural governments 
become less likely to pursue federal funding opportunities because the compliance burdens outweigh the 
potential beneﬁts. Such an outcome would ult imately reduce access to federal programs for many of the 
communities most in need of federal assistance. 
Conclusion 
Navajo County remains committed to responsible stewardship of federal funds and values its partnership 
with the federal government in serving residents throughout northeastern Arizona. 
We respectfully request that OMB: 
• Reconsider and narrow the proposed discretionary termination authority; 
• Reduce or clarify expanded pass-through entity obligations;

<!-- page 29 -->
• Limit event-services requirements to federally funded activities; 
• Provide adequate implementation periods and technical guidance; 
• Preserve meaningful stakeholder participation in future federal grant rulemaking; 
• Carefully evaluate the disproportionate impacts these changes may have on rural counties and rural 
communities. 
Thank you for the opportunity to comment on this important rulemaking. Navajo County would welcome 
the opportunity to provide additional information or discuss these concerns further. 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
 
Daryl Seymore 
Chair 
Navajo County Board of Supervisors 
Navajo County, Arizona

Re: Letter of Support Regional Cancer Access and Technology Initiative (RCATI)
To Whom It May Concern:
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors expresses its support for Summit Healthcare’s 
Regional Cancer Access and Technology Initiative (RCATI). The Navajo County Board of 
Supervisors supports improved access to quality healthcare for residents in our rural 
and frontier communities. Our constituents, including older adults, veterans, Tribal 
members, and underserved families, often face long travel distances and limited local 
options for specialty care, including cancer diagnostics and treatment.
For this reason, the Board supports efforts to expand regional access to modern cancer 
care, technology, and related services in northeastern Arizona. Initiatives that enhance 
local diagnostic capacity, treatment options, care coordination, and patient support help 
ensure that more residents can receive timely, life-saving care closer to home.
We understand that improving cancer outcomes in rural Arizona requires collaboration 
among healthcare providers, public health agencies, Tribal and local governments, and 
community partners. The Regional Cancer Access and Technology Initiative represents 
an important opportunity to strengthen this regional capacity and address long-standing 
gaps in oncology services.
Accordingly, the Navajo County Board of Supervisors offers its support for the Regional 
Cancer Access and Technology Initiative and respectfully encourages favorable 
consideration of this proposal as a means to expand healthcare access and improve 
cancer outcomes for the people of Navajo County and surrounding rural communities.
Sincerely,
Daryl Seymore

Re: Letter of Support for the First 40 Days Rural Postpartum Care Innovation Initiative
Dear Review Committee Members:
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors is pleased to express support for the 
development of the First 40 Days Rural Postpartum Care Innovation Initiative.
The Little Colorado Medical Center and 4th Trimester organizations proposed initiative 
would strengthen the rural maternal health workforce by cross-training community 
health workers as doulas and creating additional pathways for medical assistant 
students, Career and Technical Education participants, and other local residents to enter 
the maternal and child health workforce. The model would also explore how trained 
workers could provide structured in-home and community-based support during 
pregnancy, birth, and through the first 40 days after birth.
This approach has the potential to complement existing family services in Navajo County 
by extending support into families’ homes, improving connections between healthcare 
and community resources, and creating local career pathways in maternal and infant 
health. Services could include maternal wellness check-ins, education regarding 
postpartum warning signs, nutrition and food-resource navigation, lactation support, 
perinatal mental health navigation, practical assistance, and connection to appropriate 
healthcare and community services.
Access to obstetric care is limited in many of Navajo County’s rural communities, making 
it especially important to identify emerging concerns early and connect families with 
appropriate care before those concerns become emergencies. Because doula services are 
now reimbursable through AHCCCS, this initiative also offers a pathway to expand access 
to support for families at greatest risk while building a more sustainable rural 
postpartum workforce. By supporting maternal recovery, infant feeding, responsive 
caregiving, and early identification of family needs, the model also has the potential to 
improve infant and child health during this critical window of development.

<!-- page 36 -->
Navajo County
Date
2
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors supports this effort to strengthen the maternal 
health workforce and improve access to postpartum support for families across Navajo 
County. The Board appreciates the collaborative work of the participating healthcare, 
education, workforce, and community partners to develop a sustainable approach that 
responds to the needs of rural families.
Sincerely,
Daryl Seymore
Chairman 
Navajo County Board of Supervisors

Motion before the Board:
Approve the Marketing, Website Development, and Retainer Agreement with Razor Thin Media LLC. 
Background:
The Navajo County Sheriff’s Office is seeking a consultant to develop a comprehensive recruitment
marketing program centered around a fully customized, recruitment-focused WordPress website designed
to attract, engage, and convert qualified applicants. The website will serve as the primary recruitment and
information hub for the Sheriff’s Office and will showcase the department’s mission, career
opportunities, leadership, professionalism, community, and the lifestyle advantages of living and working
in Navajo County. 
The agreement includes a one-time cost of $35,000 for website development and production, a $1,500 fee
for a 5-year GoDaddy Hosting Package, and a $2,000 monthly retainer for ongoing website management
and marketing. Advertising costs are separate and will be funded by the Sheriff’s Office as needed.
